Viant Buys Waymark For $20 Million, Because CTV Needs More Ads (More!)

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Viant had a pretty good third quarter. The open internet apparently had nothing to do with it, though.

On Monday evening, the company reported just over $110 million in revenue for Q3, up 29% year over year. CTV ad spend increased almost 40% over the same time period, representing more than half of the total ad spend through Viant’s platform.

Viant also used its earnings call to announce the close of its acquisition of AI video creative production platform Waymark for $20 million in cash, a deal designed to help advertisers make more CTV ads faster.

Despite the news, Viant’s stock was down around 7% in after-hours trading. It’s not immediately clear why, but spending could be a factor. Viant expects its costs to rise about 25% next quarter, partly because of acquisitions like TVision and Waymark that aren’t expected to bring in meaningful revenue yet.

But hey, maybe that’s just the price of going all in on CTV, and Viant isn’t hedging.

Tim Vanderhook, Viant’s CEO, hyped Waymark as the start of an “era of generative ad creative for CTV” and said it could attract thousands if not millions of new advertisers to connected TV.

But where does that leave the open web? In the corner with no Patrick Swayze to save it.

During the Q&A portion of the call, Laura Martin, an analyst at Needham & Company, asked how Viant plans to keep growing revenue when the open internet is starting to shrivel like a slug in salt and even its longstanding champions, like Reddit, are throwing in the towel.

In response, Tim Vanderhook said that the open internet is only “10% or less” of Viant’s business and is “certainly not driving the growth rates we’re seeing now.”

As far as Viant is concerned, every dollar leaving the open web is a dollar headed toward CTV.

Advertisers are shifting more of their budgets away from display and online video towards more addressable and “premium” environments, Vanderhook said, which he believes will “end up being a catalyst for us next year.”

Still, just because budgets are moving to CTV, doesn’t mean every advertiser can afford to make a TV ad – and that’s where Waymark comes in.

Waymark’s software turns a brand’s inputs into a finished video ad in a matter of minutes, complete with a custom script, visuals and voiceover. Local media sellers, including Spectrum Reach, Comcast, NBCU and Fox already use it to produce linear and CTV spots for local advertisers.

In Viant’s view, Waymark addresses three problems: the high cost of producing TV-quality ads, creative fatigue and ads that aren’t tailored to the audience.

Production costs keep most small and midsize advertisers out of CTV, said Viant’s COO, Chris Vanderhook, and so “the down market opportunity is incredible.”

But big enterprise advertisers also need more creative. They run the same ads over and over again until people just tune them out, Tim Vanderhook said.

“It’s not that the creative is bad,” he said, “it’s that they do a big production shoot, they have that creative in market for three to six months and users are overfrequented.”

Viant’s TVision panel data suggests that when someone has seen an ad too many times, their attention plummets, but fresh creative can increase their attention by more than 80%.

The final part of the equation is targeting. Viant plans to pair Waymark with its Household ID, Iris Content ID and TVision’s attention data to tailor different creative versions to specific audiences and also to what they’re watching.

Take an online university that’s trying to reach prospective students. Instead of running a single ad for everyone, the advertiser could have one about athletics, another about academics, a third about campus life and so on.

Viant would use its Household ID to match an ad to the right audience (sports fans, say, or academically minded prospects). The content ID would contextually target the ad to whatever’s on the screen (the athletics ad during a sports game and the academic ad during an ad break in a documentary). And then TVision could measure attention.

Chris Vanderhook referred to this process as “the transformation of creative from a fixed asset into a dynamic asset.”

Even so, for now at least, Waymark is more of a bet than a business. Viant expects it to add little revenue this quarter or next year. But the deal does fit the pattern. Viant is going for those CTV dollars.

“The paradigm is shifting,” Tim Vanderhook said. “TV is no longer reserved only for the world’s largest brands.”

TIME is using AI agents to draft proposals and troubleshoot campaigns – but not to replace the human relationships that drive ad sales.

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

The Story Behind Why Smallville’s Ending Didn’t Reveal Clark In The Superman Suit

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

NCIS' Latest Celebrity Cameo Was A Classic Callback, But It Confused Me For Most Of The Episode

James Gunn Would Like You To Know He’s Not Trying To Recreate The MCU (Even With Superman)

I Can't Believe Tom Welling Told A Wild Nude Fan Pic Story The Same Weekend His Own Pic Went Viral

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It's a bird... It's a plane... It's not Tom Welling in tights...

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Before the Arrowverse existed, fans had a Superman show without Superman called Smallville, and that series defined my childhood. Now, it’s celebrating it’s 25th anniversary (and making me feel old). The series focuses squarely on Clark Kent, and it makes some interesting choices regarding the DC mythos. Interestingly, the show never saw Tom Welling's Clark fully suited up as the Man of Steel, and the actor himself shared some BTS details about why that was the case.

Just to refresh fans' memories, Clark never wore the classic red-and-blue suit, and barely hit the skies. However, the final moment gave a little wink at who this fan-favorite Clark Kent had become by showing him running across the rooftop of the Daily Planet and ripping open his shirt to reveal the classic S on the chest. This was the closest fans came to seeing the actor in the hero tights.

When Welling and his co-star, Michael Rosenbaum, (who played Lex Luther) spoke with Variety, he revealed the reason behind the lack of a super suit:

We got to set that day, and they had given me just a T-shirt with no sleeves to wear underneath the shirt. Because they didn’t have the money to put the suit on, and they weren’t going to see it anyway.

The absence of a full suit wasn’t a major surprise, given creators Alfred Gough and Miles Millar initiated a “no flights, no tights” rule when they started the show. Only one of those was ever broken. While Welling admits that they “toed the line a few times” when it came to the suit, they never did get the actor in a pair of tights. The reason for that was pretty simple. They weren’t able to do so unless Welling gave the “all right,” and to this day, this iteration of Clark has not suited up.

Funny enough, the closest we came to seeing Clark in a hero costume was when he wore the Green Arrow suit for an episode. Years later, along with Erica Durance (who played Lois Lane), Welling did reprise his role as Clark for a scene in the Arrowverse's "Crisis on Infinite Earths” crossover event, though even by that point, the character had given up his powers and retired as a hero. Despite Kent not taking to the skies in the final shot, Welling enjoyed the series, and he had this to say about his character's status:

We can’t go with him. But he’s out there.

This is technically true, and there have also been plenty of Supermen before and after Smallville. Yet fans have remained dedicated to the series. That's partially thanks to Welling, Rosenbaum, and several cast members reuniting conventions around the country. Plus, Rosenbaum and Welling host the re-watch podcast “Talkville,” which is currently recapping Season 9. With that, fans continue to hear BTS stories and re-live their favorite moments.

While there have been other Supes shows, the former WB/CW series still stands as the only series to solely focus on Clark and not his alter ego. But this could change if Welling and Rosenbaum succeed in reviving the series. The duo have been reportedly attempting to bring back Smallville via an animated sequel, and this could potentially mean (finally) seeing Clark in the suit.

I'd still actually like to see Tom Welling don the costume in a live-action production, though, even if the chances of that happening seem slim to none. At the very least, fans can enjoy Clark's entire journey by streaming all 10 seasons of Smallville with a Hulu subscription.

Netflix's 4-Season Dark Fantasy Series Was This Close To Greatness

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Netflix really had something great on its hands with its four-season dark fantasy series, but it fell short in the very last moments. The world of television is already pretty unforgiving, and the streaming landscape has made it even harder for projects to stand out amid a sea of content. The fantasy genre doesn't make things any easier, since it's all too common for these shows to flop after only a single season if they don't check all the right boxes. It's for all these reasons that it's rather remarkable when anything sticks.

One of these remarkable TV shows is Netflix's The Umbrella Academy. The show premiered in 2019 and pretty quickly proved itself to be unlike any other fantasy series on screens. The Umbrella Academy is deliciously dark, but this is layered up with a quirky, character-driven narrative that aims to subvert the superhero genre at every turn. This isn't an ordinary Marvel or MCU show. Instead, Umbrella Academy is all about complicated families.

The story of The Umbrella Academy revolves around a group of seven adopted children with miraculous abilities who are raised as a superhero team, known, of course, as the Umbrella Academy. This family ultimately fractured and dissolved, but the estranged siblings find their reunion as adults following their adoptive father's death. The show is all time travel and apocalypse-level events, layered with family dysfunction and character quirks. For its first three seasons, The Umbrella Academy was on top of the world. Unfortunately, season 4 is when things collapsed.

It's important to note that, otherwise, The Umbrella Academy is an excellent series that is still entirely worth watching. Unfortunately, there is really no denying that the fourth and final season really missed the mark. While seasons 2 and 3 both earned impressive 91% scores on Rotten Tomatoes, season 4 came in shockingly low at a "rotten" 55%. Something went wrong, and critics had no trouble pointing out exactly what.

First things first, The Umbrella Academy season 4 suffered because of its rushed narrative. While previous installments were made up of 10 episodes, season 4 had only six. It simply wasn't enough time to meaningfully wrap up the established story, but things got even worse when the episodes seemed to focus on all the wrong things. Beloved characters were misused or abandoned, tired plot devices were repeated, and the final big twist of Umbrella Academy felt, to many, like a slap to the face. Overall, it was just unfortunate.

The fact that, despite all of this, The Umbrella Academy is still an easy show to recommend is really a testament to the excellence of those first few seasons, especially seasons 2 and 3. It is perhaps better to know ahead of time that the ending won't satisfy the way many would have hoped. However, if you hope to give this fantasy series a try, keep in mind that the ending may still be revised.

Like many great superhero TV shows, The Umbrella Academy is based on a comic book series. Like many other great fantasy TV shows, The Umbrella Academy got ahead of its source material. This meant that Netflix's writers were on their own for season 4 and its ending, on top of the disadvantage of having to wrap things up in six episodes. The good news is that future installments of The Umbrella Academy are on the way, with original creators Gerard Way and Gabriel Bá continuing to put out new content.

Since The Umbrella Academy came to its unfortunate close in 2024, the comics have proceeded with several new issues, the most recent having been released on October 28, 2026. The series finale, issue #6, is expected to hit shelves near the beginning of 2027. This will definitively wrap up the story as it was intended to be, and there will be absolutely no need to rush through in the way Netflix did. So, if you get hooked on The Umbrella Academy, take comfort in the knowledge that there is still light at the end of the tunnel.

2027 Will Give One Piece A New Lead Hero, And It's Not Luffy

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

For nearly three decades, Monkey D. Luffy has fronted One Piece, and its theatrical adventures have usually followed the Straw Hat captain or put his crew in a new crisis. That pattern is set to change in 2027. One Piece Film: God Valley explores a chapter of history that unfolded long before Luffy began his voyage.

The title points toward the infamous incident involving Gol D. Roger, Monkey D. Garp, and Rocks D. Xebec. Those three figures appeared in the official announcement teaser, signaling a different generation of heroes and threats. Luffy is not being replaced in the main series; this movie simply has a past-era story to tell.

Toei Animation announced One Piece Film: God Valley on August 23, 2026, during One Piece Day '26, confirming a Japanese theatrical release in summer 2027. The teaser offered no full synopsis, but its focus on Roger, Garp, and Rocks makes the historical setting unmistakable. This is not another adventure built around the Straw Hat crew.

The God Valley Incident took place 38 years before One Piece's present-day events. Luffy's voyage begins in a world shaped by its aftermath, not in the era when these legends were making history. A film set during that conflict naturally shifts attention to the people who fought there, rather than the pirate who inherited their legacy.

Roger is a compelling candidate to anchor the story. He was not yet the Pirate King remembered by the world, and his rivalry with Rocks preceded the final stretch of his journey to the One Piece. The teaser places him alongside Garp, suggesting the movie will explore the unlikely partnership that helped define the incident.

The island gives the story a darker foundation than a conventional treasure hunt. In the manga, God Valley was the site of a World Noble hunting competition, in which the island's people were treated as prey. The arrival of powerful pirates and Marines turned that atrocity into a battle whose truth the World Government later tried to erase.

Garp's role complicates the idea of a single central hero. A Marine, he joined forces with Roger, a pirate, during the crisis. The official version of events elevated Garp as the Hero of the Marines while concealing Roger's part in the battle. A film can dramatize the partnership behind a story the authorities deliberately distorted.

Rocks D. Xebec is not simply a name for Roger and Garp to defeat. He led the Rocks Pirates, a crew that included younger versions of future powerhouses such as Edward Newgate, Charlotte Linlin, and Kaido. The manga's extended flashback also gives Rocks personal history and motivations, making him central to the tragedy rather than a faceless villain.

God Valley appears set apart because its title points to a major event in Eiichiro Oda's manga, rather than another separate Straw Hat adventure. Earlier films have largely told original stories, while this project seems poised to bring a defining piece of the series' history to theaters. That could make its different lead perspective more than a casting change.

The timing adds to the film's importance and excitement. One Piece began serialization on July 22, 1997, so its 30th anniversary falls on July 22, 2027, the same year God Valley is scheduled to arrive. It will also be the franchise's first new theatrical feature since One Piece Film: Red opened in Japan in August 2022.

Weekly One Piece episodes are halting indefinitely, but the anime hiatus clears the runway for a major canon movie.

Toei has not revealed the director, screenplay, voice cast, exact Japanese premiere date, or international release plans. The studio has not formally identified one character as the protagonist, either. Roger may be the clearest candidate for the One Piece film's heroic point of view, but Garp and Rocks are essential to the story.

‘Paddington: The Musical’ Sets Broadway Cast With Tony Hale, J. Quinton Johnson, Jessie Mueller & More

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Paddington: The Musical, which opens in 2027, has set its cast, featuring Arrested Development’s Tony Hale in his Broadway debut.

The musical will be led by Kelvin Dukes (America’s Got Talent) as Tony, Drew Gehling (Waitress) as Mr. Brown, Allison Guinn (Operation Mincemeat) as Lady Sloane and Hale (Veep) as Mr. Curry.

Arti Shah, who originated the role of Paddington on the West End, will reprise on Broadway, with James Hameed also returning as Paddington’s voice and remote puppetry.

The cast of the musical also includes J. Quinton Johnson (Hamilton) as Grant, Leslie Rodriguez Kritzer (The Fantasticks) as Millicent Clyde, Zonya Love (The Color Purple) as Tanya, Jessie Mueller (Waitress) as Mrs. Brown, Serena Parrish (Don’t Say Good Luck) as Judy Brown, Karen Ziemba (Contact) as Mrs. Bird and Chip Zien (Into the Woods original Broadway cast) as Mr. Gruber. Watch on Deadline

Paddington: The Musical will begin previews on Tuesday, March 30, at Broadway’s Al Hirschfeld Theatre ahead of its opening night on Sunday, April 18.

Based on A Bear Called Paddington, written by Michael Bond, and the film Paddington, the musical features music and lyrics by Tom Fletcher, a book by Jessica Swale and direction by Luke Sheppard.

In Paddington: The Musical, a small, lost bear from Peru arrives in London in search of a place to call home. A chance encounter with the Browns leads him into an unexpected world of adventure, where kindness has the power to change lives and strangers can become family. But London isn’t all afternoon tea and friendly faces — and even the seemingly happiest of families can have cracks beneath the surface. So when a mysterious and vengeful villain sets her sights on Paddington, the Browns embark on a thrilling rescue mission, realizing they need this special bear as much as he needs them.

Global carbon emissions from fossil fuels are projected to decrease by 0.4% in 2026—driven by expansion of solar and wind power and the Iran conflict—according to new research by the Global Carbon Project. Schroders Emerging-Market Bond Strategy Moves Onto Solana Through Centrifuge

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Schroders is bringing an emerging-market bond strategy into the tokenized fund market through Centrifuge on Solana. The November 4 announcement concerns the Schroder International Selection Fund Emerging Market Bond strategy, with Centrifuge providing onchain infrastructure.

Schroders is bringing an emerging-market bond strategy into the tokenized fund market through Centrifuge on Solana. The November 4 announcement concerns the Schroder International Selection Fund Emerging Market Bond strategy, with Centrifuge providing onchain infrastructure. It is notable because the assets are not a cryptocurrency basket: investors are being offered a blockchain-native route to a conventional, actively managed fixed-income strategy with its own risks and investment processes.

Centrifuge says the initiative will introduce tokenized exposure to the Schroders emerging-market bond fund. Schroders manages more than $1 trillion in assets across its business, according to the announcement, and has decades of experience investing in debt issued by emerging-market governments and companies. That corporate scale provides context for the partnership; it is not an estimate of how much capital has entered this particular onchain fund. The launch represents Schroders' first Solana deployment, a specific milestone that should not be confused with a wholesale shift of the manager's portfolios to public blockchains.

Emerging-market bonds can offer access to currencies, credit risks and interest-rate conditions very different from those facing US Treasury bills. The underlying portfolio may be affected by sovereign finances, corporate defaults, currency movements and changes in global borrowing costs. Tokenization does not eliminate those exposures. It changes how interests in an investment strategy can be issued, recorded, distributed or serviced. Investors need to understand the fund's legal structure, eligibility, fees, dealing arrangements and the precise way token holdings map to recognized ownership. Those details ultimately determine whether the product meets their requirements.

Solana's appeal to financial product developers includes its transaction throughput and low per-transaction fees, while Centrifuge focuses on the specialist infrastructure needed to operate tokenized funds. The challenge is making asset management routines work with a public network without losing compliance controls. Fund subscriptions, redemptions, transfer restrictions and investor records must align with the legally constituted vehicle. A fast chain is helpful, but it cannot replace the fund administrator, custodian or manager. Operational success depends on the quality of those connections, not simply on the number of transactions the blockchain can process.

Much of the early RWA market concentrated on tokenized cash and short-dated US government debt. An actively managed emerging-market bond strategy is a different proposition because valuation, liquidity and portfolio turnover can be more complicated. A market may be open at all hours even when underlying bonds trade within narrower windows. That difference makes pricing and redemption policies essential. The value proposition could be broader distribution and improved processing, but investors should not infer that every share can be redeemed instantly or that tokenization creates round-the-clock liquidity in the underlying securities.

The next useful evidence would include an initial live issuance, assets held in the tokenized structure, participating distributors, supported jurisdictions and how subscriptions and redemptions actually settle. Over time, the question is whether blockchain infrastructure improves operational efficiency and attracts new investors without weakening fund protections. The announcement is a meaningful institutional experiment, but the practical success of the fund will rest on ordinary asset-management disciplines as much as on Solana technology.

Primary source: Centrifuge official product announcement. For additional context, see NewsBTC’s report on Solana’s infrastructure and institutional RWA growth.
